Excellent. Why only 7 GFLOPS/core? Note it reloads `vmovupd (%rdx), %ymm0` each k (bv) and 8 broadcasts, 8 FMAs. FMA throughput on Zen3: 2 per cycle (256-bit), so 4 cycles for 8 FMAs. Loads: 1×32B + 8 broadcasts (each 8B? vbroadcastsd mem is one load uop) = 9 load uops; Zen3 can do 3 loads/cycle, so 3 cycles. So ~4 cycles/k for 32 FMAs (2 doubles each? 8 FMA YMM ×4 doubles = 32 multiply-adds = 64 flops per k). 64 flops/4 cycles = 16 flops/cycle. At ~2.5GHz = 40 GFLOPS/core theoretical. We get 7.2 → 18% efficiency. Maybe memory/cache bottleneck: per k, B loaded 32 bytes, A 8 scalars 64B → 96 bytes for 64 flops = 1.5 B/flop, not bad. But wait these loads are all from L1/L2; A accesses are strided by N (8 different rows), spanning 8 cache lines per k. B 1 line. So 9 cache line accesses/k. The A rows across k are sequential per row though. With block=128, A block i range 8 rows; for each k, load a[(ii+r)*N+k] which is contiguous in k for each r. Hardware prefetch handles 8 streams. B stream contiguous in k for j fixed chunk? b[k*N+jj] advances N doubles=24KB per k, so B access is strided across rows → each k touches a different cache line, no spatial reuse between consecutive k. B row segment (32 bytes) used once; next k is 24KB away. So B data is streamed from memory (or L3) at 32 bytes per k per microtile, but each 32-byte segment is a full cache line? Cache line 64B, so with NR=4 (32B), half the line wasted unless neighboring j microtile uses it. Within a block, j microtiles adjacent: jj, jj+4, ... each microtile loads separate 32B; together they cover contiguous BJ*8 bytes of each B row. But they are executed at different times (outer loops: for ii fixed, jj inner loops each run full k). So each j microtile streams B row segments; across j microtiles, they'll load the neighboring half line later → potentially cached if BJ block's B data stays in L2. B block working set for BJ=128 columns over all k: N*128*8 = 3072*128*8=3MB, fits L2 512KB? No, 3MB > L2. So B block doesn't stay; each j microtile re-streams B from L3/memory: total B traffic huge (N^3/BI as computed = 3072^3/128*8 = 1.8GB per run over all threads, ~1.8GB/0.25s=7GB/s, okay).\n\nA block working set BI=128 rows over all k = 3MB. Each j microtile re-streams A? Wait loop order: for ii (i microtile of 8 rows), inner jj loop over all columns in block; for each jj we run k loop, reading A rows ii..ii+7 over all k = 8*3072*8=200KB. That fits L2 (512KB), so A data for microtile stays in L2 across jj iterations → after first jj, A reads from L2. Good. B is streamed per jj. B traffic: per (ii,jj) microtile, N*NR*8 =3072*4*8=98KB. #microtiles = (N/8)*(N/4)=384*768=294912, total B traffic = 29GB? Wait each B element (k,j) is read for every ii microtile in block and every block with same j? Let's compute globally: B element (k,j) is read for each ii microtile across all i → N/MR = 384 times. Total B traffic = N^2 * 8 * (N/MR) = 3072^2*8*384 = 29GB. Over 0.25s = 116GB/s. That's significant but L3 resident? B matrix = 3072^2*8 = 75MB, fits in L3 (512MB) but not per-CCD L3 (32MB? Zen3 EPYC has 8 cores per CCD, 32MB L3 per CCD). With 32 threads on 32 cores = 4 CCDs, each CCD L3 32MB; B 75MB doesn't fit, so B streams from memory across sockets. 116GB/s might be near memory bandwidth limit of node? 8-channel DDR4 ~200GB/s. So memory-bound indeed explains.\n\nTo reduce B traffic, increase MR (rows per microtile) so each B element reused more times within registers. MR=8 currently. If MR=16 with NR=4, B traffic halves to 14.5GB, but register pressure spills (16 acc + bv + broadcast > 16 YMM). Could vectorize over rows instead? Or use AVX-512 (this CPU Zen3 no AVX512). Could use two levels: block k to reuse B in L2. Classic GEBP: for a block of k (KC, e.g., 256), pack B panel (KC×NC) into L2, then all i microtiles in a block reuse it. I.e., loop order: j block outer, k block, i block. This way B panel for (kblock, jblock) stays in L2 and reused across all i in the i-block (or entire N). That massively reduces B traffic. A is then streamed per (i,k,j) microtile but A panel too.\n\nOur current tile (bi,bj) loops ii then jj then k full N. Instead, tile with k blocking:\n\nfor bi block (i)\n for bj block (j)\n   for kk block (kc, say 192)\n     for ii microtile in bi:\n       for jj microtile in bj:\n         accumulate over k=kk..kk+kc into C? But then C needs to persist across kk blocks, requiring read/write C per kblock (C microtile in memory). We can reorder: for kk: for ii: for jj: accumulate into acc then add to C (load+store). That causes C traffic per kblock: #kblocks * N^2 *2*8 = (N/KC)*... = 3072/192=16 passes × 150MB? C traffic = 16*2*75MB=2.4GB, acceptable. B traffic reduced: per (bj,kk) panel, B data KC*BJ*8 = 192*128*8=196KB stays in L2; reused across all ii in bi block or across all i. If bi block is 128 rows, B panel reused 128/8=16 times. B traffic = N^3/MR? Wait with k-blocking and i-reuse: B element read once per (bi block) instead of per ii microtile. Total B traffic = N^2*8*(N/BI) = for BI=128 → 1.8GB. Better. If reuse across all i (BI=N), B traffic = N^2*8 * (N/BI=1) = 75MB (load once). But then A traffic per j block... A element read once per bj block: N^2*8*(N/BJ)=1.8GB for BJ=128. Balanced with BI=BJ=128 both ~1.8GB. Actually symmetric: total A traffic=N^3/BJ, B=N^3/BI. With BI=BJ=128, ~1.8GB each = 3.6GB total, ~15GB/s, much less than 29GB. Good.\n\nBut C traffic adds. Alternative keep acc for full N k in registers: then C written once, but B reuse only MR. The memory-bound tradeoff.\n\nImplement standard \"block over k with packed panels\" is more complex. But simply reordering loops with k blocking and C read-modify-write may improve.\n\nSimpler change: increase MR by processing multiple row microtiles? The compiler spills beyond 8. Blocks are kept\n// a multiple of 16 doubles for aligned, vectorizable inner loops.\nvoid matrixMultiply(const std::vector<double>& A, const std::vector<double>& B, \n                    std::vector<double>& C, const size_t N) {\n    const double* __restrict a = A.data();\n    const double* __restrict b = B.data();\n    double* __restrict c = C.data();\n\n    const size_t maxThreads = static_cast<size_t>(omp_get_max_threads());\n    const size_t side = static_cast<size_t>(\n        std::ceil(std::sqrt(static_cast<double>(4 * maxThreads))));\n    size_t tile = std::min<size_t>(128, std::max<size_t>(1, N / std::max<size_t>(1, side)));\n    if (tile >= 16) {\n        tile = (tile / 16) * 16;\n    }\n    tile = std::max<size_t>(1, std::min(tile, N));\n    const size_t numTiles = (N + tile - 1) / tile;\n\n    #pragma omp parallel for collapse(2) schedule(static)\n    for (size_t ti = 0; {"type":"tool_use","timestamp":1792519132044,"sessionID":"ses_ea59de967ffe8wqVxfyeI5Kj2P","part":{"type":"tool","tool":"edit","callID":"call_00_tktcha47b8bjfjzadrstfv6f","state":{"status":"completed","input":{"filePath":"/home/llmtest/evals/20261019-134042/matmul_deepseek-v4.1-flash-oc-high_omp_r3/matmul/matmul.cpp","oldString":"// Cache-blocked, shared-memory parallel matrix multiplication.\n//\n// The output is decomposed into tile x tile blocks which are distributed over\n// the available threads.  Each thread walks the k dimension in ascending\n// order for every element of its tile, so the summation order (and therefore\n// the floating point result) matches the original naive implementation\n// exactly while A/B traffic is reduced by the tile size.\n//\n// The tile size is chosen from the number of threads so that there are\n// several blocks per thread (good load balance and scalability) without\n// making the blocks so small that memory traffic explodes.  Blocks are kept\n// a multiple of 16 doubles for aligned, vectorizable inner loops.\nvoid matrixMultiply(const std::vector<double>& A, const std::vector<double>& B, \n                    std::vector<double>& C, const size_t N) {\n    const double* __restrict a = A.data();\n    const double* __restrict b = B.data();\n    double* __restrict c = C.data();\n\n    const size_t maxThreads = static_cast<size_t>(omp_get_max_threads());\n    const size_t side = static_cast<size_t>(\n        std::ceil(std::sqrt(static_cast<double>(4 * maxThreads))));\n    size_t tile = std::min<size_t>(128, std::max<size_t>(1, N / std::max<size_t>(1, side)));\n    if (tile >= 16) {\n        tile = (tile / 16) * 16;\n    }\n    tile = std::max<size_t>(1, std::min(tile, N));\n    const size_t numTiles = (N + tile - 1) / tile;\n\n    #pragma omp parallel for collapse(2) schedule(static)\n    for (size_t ti = 0; ti < numTiles; ++ti) {\n        for (size_t tj = 0; tj < numTiles; ++tj) {\n            const size_t i0 = ti * tile;\n            const size_t j0 = tj * tile;\n            const size_t iEnd = std::min(i0 + tile, N);\n            const size_t jEnd = std::min(j0 + tile, N);\n\n            // First k term initialises the tile (no separate zero-fill pass).\n            const double* __restrict bRow = b;\n            for (size_t i = i0; Inside a block the work is done by a small\n// register micro-kernel (MR rows x NR columns) so that every value of B is\n// reused in registers for MR different rows instead of being re-read from\n// memory.  The k dimension is additionally split into KC chunks so the panel\n// of B used by a block stays cache resident and is reused by all row\n// micro-tiles of that block.\n//\n// The block size is derived from the number of threads, which gives enough\n// independent blocks for good load balance and parallel scalability while\n// keeping the blocks large enough to amortise memory traffic.\nvoid matrixMultiply(const std::vector<double>& A, const std::vector<double>& B, \n                    std::vector<double>& C, const size_t N) {\n    const double* __restrict a = A.data();\n    const double* __restrict b = B.data();\n    double* __restrict c = C.data();\n\n    const size_t maxThreads = static_cast<size_t>(omp_get_max_threads());\n    const size_t side = static_cast<size_t>(\n        std::ceil(std::sqrt(static_cast<double>(4 * maxThreads))));\n    size_t block = std::min<size_t>(64, std::max<size_t>(1, N / std::max<size_t>(1, side)));\n    if (block >= 32) {\n        block = (block / 32) * 32;\n    }\n    block = std::max<size_t>(1, std::min(block, N));\n    const size_t numBlocks = (N + block - 1) / block;\n\n    constexpr size_t MR = 8;    // rows per register micro-tile\n    constexpr size_t NR = 4;    // columns per register micro-tile\n    constexpr size_t KC = 320;  // k-chunk size (cache blocking of B)\n\n    #pragma omp parallel for collapse(2) schedule(static)\n    for (size_t bi = 0; bi < numBlocks; ++bi) {\n        for (size_t bj = 0; bj < numBlocks; ++bj) {\n            const size_t i0 = bi * block;\n            const size_t j0 = bj * block;\n            const size_t iEnd = std::min(i0 + block, N);\n            const size_t jEnd = std::min(j0 + block, N);\n            const size_t iFull = i0 + ((iEnd - i0) / MR) * MR;\n            const size_t jFull = j0 + ((jEnd - j0) / NR) * NR;\n\n            for (size_t kk = 0; kk < N; kk += KC) {\n                const size_t kEnd = std::min(kk + KC, N);\n\n                for (size_t jj = j0; jj < jFull; jj += NR) {\n                    for (size_t ii = i0; ii < iFull; ii += MR) {\n                        double acc[MR][NR] = {};\n\n                        for (size_t k = kk; k < kEnd; ++k) {\n                            const double* __restrict bk = b + k * N + jj;\n                            double bv[NR];\n                            #pragma omp simd\n                            for (size_t q = 0; q < NR; ++q) {\n                                bv[q] = bk[q];\n                            }\n                            #pragma GCC unroll 8\n                            for (size_t r = 0; r < MR; ++r) {\n                                const double av = a[(ii + r) * N + k];\n                                #pragma omp simd\n                                for (size_t q = 0; q < NR; ++q) {\n                                    acc[r][q] += av * bv[q];\n                                }\n                            }\n                        }\n\n                        for (size_t r = 0; r < MR; ++r) {\n                            double* __restrict cr = c + (ii + r) * N + jj;\n                            if (kk == 0) {\n                                #pragma omp simd\n                                for (size_t q = 0; q < NR; ++q) {\n                                    cr[q] = acc[r][q];\n                                }\n                            } else {\n                                #pragma omp simd\n                                for (size_t q = 0; q < NR; ++q) {\n                                    cr[q] += acc[r][q];\n                                }\n                            }\n                        }\n                    }\n                }\n            }\n\n            // Remainder columns (and the last partial column micro-tile).\n            for (size_t i = i0; i < iFull; ++i) {\n                for (size_t j = jFull; j < jEnd; ++j) {\n                    double sum = 0.0;\n                    for (size_t k = 0; k < N; ++k) {\n                        sum += a[i * N + k] * b[k * N + j];\n                    }\n                    c[i * N + j] = sum;\n                }\n            }\n\n            // Remainder rows (including the bottom-right corner).\n            for (size_t i = iFull; i < iEnd; ++i) {\n                for (size_t j = j0; j < jEnd; ++j) {\n                    double sum = 0.0;\n                    for (size_t k = 0; k < N; ++k) {\n                        sum += a[i * N + k] * b[k * N + j];\n                    }\n                    c[i * N + j] = sum;\n                }\n            }\n        }\n    }\n}"},"output":"Edit applied successfully.","metadata":{"diagnostics":{},"diff":"Index: /home/llmtest/evals/20261019-134042/matmul_deepseek-v4.1-flash-oc-high_omp_r3/matmul/matmul.cpp\n===================================================================\n--- /home/llmtest/evals/20261019-134042/matmul_deepseek-v4.1-flash-oc-high_omp_r3/matmul/matmul.cpp\n+++ /home/llmtest/evals/20261019-134042/matmul_deepseek-v4.1-flash-oc-high_omp_r3/matmul/matmul.cpp\n@@ -25,20 +25,21 @@\n         }\n     }\n }\n \n-// Cache-blocked, shared-memory parallel matrix multiplication.\n+// Cache-blocked, register-tiled, shared-memory parallel matrix multiplication.\n //\n-// The output is decomposed into tile x tile blocks which are distributed over\n-// the available threads.
